I need to determine the equation of a parabola with a vertex at (0, 0) and a focus at (0, 4). 

First, I'll identify the standard form of a vertical parabola. Since the axis of symmetry is vertical, the equation will be in the form \( y = ax^2 \).

Next, I'll find the value of 'a' using the relationship between the vertex and the focus. For a vertical parabola, the distance from the vertex to the focus is given by \( p = \frac{1}{4a} \). Here, the focus is at (0, 4), so \( p = 4 \).

Solving for 'a', I rearrange the equation: 
\( a = \frac{1}{4p} = \frac{1}{4 \times 4} = \frac{1}{16} \).

Finally, substituting 'a' back into the standard form gives the equation of the parabola as \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).
</think>

To find the equation of a parabola with a **vertex** at (0, 0) and a **focus** at (0, 4), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Direction:**
   - Since the focus is above the vertex on the y-axis, the parabola opens **upward**.

2. **Determine the Standard Form:**
   - The standard equation for a parabola opening upward with vertex at (h, k) is:
     \[
     (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
     \]
     where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

3. **Plug in Known Values:**
   - Here, \( h = 0 \), \( k = 0 \), and \( p = 4 \).

4. **Substitute into the Equation:**
   \[
   (x - 0)^2 = 4(4)(y - 0)
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   x^2 = 16y
   \]

5. **Final Equation:**
   The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is \boxed{y = \dfrac{x^2}{16}}

The standard form of a vertical parabola with vertex \((h, k)\) and coefficient \(a\) is:

\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]

Given:
- Vertex at (5, -2): so \(h = 5\) and \(k = -2\).
- Focus at (5, -4).

For a vertical parabola, the focus is located at \((h, k + p)\), where \(p\) is the distance from the vertex to the focus. Since the parabola opens downward, \(p\) is negative.

The relationship between \(a\) and \(p\) for a vertical parabola is:

\[
a = \frac{1}{4p}
\]

Given that the focus is 2 units below the vertex (from y = -2 to y = -4):

\[
p = -2
\]

Substitute into the equation for \(a\):

\[
a = \frac{1}{4(-2)} = -\frac{1}{8}
\]

Now, substitute \(h\), \(k\), and \(a\) back into the standard form:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 5)^2 - 2
\]

This is the equation of the parabola.

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the parabola is \(\boxed{y=-\frac{1}{8}(x-5)^2-2}\).

To find the **vertex** of the parabola given by the equation:

\[
y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
\]

we can use the formula for the vertex of a quadratic function in standard form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \). The vertex coordinates are given by:

\[
(h, k) = \left( -\frac{b}{2a}, f\left(-\frac{b}{2a}\right) \right)
\]

From the equation:
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)

\[
h = -\frac{b}{2a} = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -1
\]

**Step 3: Calculate the y-coordinate of the vertex (\( k \))**

Substitute \( x = -1 \) into the original equation:

\[
k = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2

The given equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) can be rewritten in vertex form by completing the square. 

First, rearrange and complete the square for the \( x \)-terms:
\[
x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12
\]
Add 4 to both sides to complete the square:
\[
x^2 - 4x + 4 = -8y - 12 + 4
\]
This simplifies to:
\[
(x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)
\]
Dividing both sides by \(-8\) gives the vertex form:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: } y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1}
\]

The problem involves finding the equation of a parabola with vertex at (0,3) and directrix y=3. 

To analyze this:

1. **Vertex Form**: For a vertical parabola opening upwards or downwards, the standard form is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
   \]
   where (h,k) is the vertex.

2. **Given Values**: Here, \( h = 0 \), \( k = 3 \). So,
   \[
   x^2 = 4p(y - 3)
   \]

3. **Directrix Equation**: For a vertical parabola opening upwards, the directrix is:
   \[
   y = k - p
   \]
   Given that the directrix is \( y = 3 \),
   \[
   3 = 3 - p \implies p = 0
   \]

4. **Conclusion**: Since \( p = 0 \), the parabola collapses into a straight line, which isn't possible for a standard parabola.

Thus, there is no valid non-degenerate parabola with the given conditions.

\[
\boxed{\text{No such parabola exists}}
\]

The given equation \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \) is a standard form of a vertical parabola.

- **Vertex**: The vertex is at \( (h, k) = (1, 5) \).

To find the focus and directrix:
- Compare with the standard form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \).
- Here, \( a = 1 \), \( h = 1 \), and \( k = 5 \).

**Focus Calculation:**
The formula for the focus is \( (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \):
\[ y_{\text{focus}} = 5 + \frac{1}{4(1)} = 5.25 \]
So, the focus is at \( (1, 5.25) \) or as a fraction, \( (1, \frac{21}{4}) \).

**Directrix Calculation:**
The directrix equation is \( y = k - \frac{1}{4a} \):
\[ y_{\text{directrix}} = 5 - \frac{1}{4(1)} = 4.75 \]
So, the directrix is at \( y = 4.75 \) or as a fraction, \( y = \frac{19}{4} \).

**Final Answer:**
- Vertex: (1, 5)
- Focus: (1, 21/4)
- Directrix: y = 19/4

Starting with the first point (2,8). If I plug x=2 and y=8 into the equation, it becomes:
8 = a*(2)² + b*2 + c
Which simplifies to 8 = 4a + 2b + c. Let's call this Equation 1.

Next is (-1,3). Plugging x=-1 and y=3 into the general form gives:
3 = a*(-1)² + b*(-1) + c
Simplifying that: 3 = a - b + c. That's Equation 2.

Lastly, the point (0,4). Plugging x=0 and y=4 in:
4 = a*(0)² + b*0 + c
Which simplifies to 4 = c. So we immediately know that c is 4. Let me note that down: c = 4.

Now that I have c, I can substitute it into the other equations to reduce the number of variables. Starting with Equation 1:
8 = 4a + 2b + c
But since c=4, this becomes:
8 = 4a + 2b + 4
Subtracting 4 from both sides gives:
4 = 4a + 2b
Which simplifies to 2 = 2a + b. Let's call this Equation 1a.

Now for Equation 2:
3 = a - b + c
Again, substituting c=4:
3 = a - b + 4
Subtracting 4 from both sides gives:
-1 = a - b

Now we have two equations with two variables (a and b):
Equation 1a: 2a + b = 2
Equation 2a: a - b = -1

I can solve these simultaneously. Maybe by adding them to eliminate b. Adding the left sides:
(2a + b) + (a - b) = 3a
Adding the right sides:
2 + (-1) = 1

So, 3a = 1 => a = 1/3.

Now that I have a, I can plug it back into one of the equations to find b. Let's use Equation 2a:
(1/3) - b = -1
Subtracting (1/3) from both sides gives:
-b = -1 - 1/3 => -b = -4/3

Multiplying both sides by -1: b = 4/3.

So now, I have a=1/3, b=4/3, and c=4. Plugging these back into the general equation gives:
y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4

First point (2,8):
Calculate y: (1/3)(4) + (4/3)(2) + 4 = 4/3 + 8/3 + 4 = (12/3) + 4 = 4 + 4 = 8. Correct.

Second point (-1,3):
y = (1/3)(1) + (4/3)(-1) + 4 = (1/3 - 4/3) +4= (-3/3)+4= -1+4=3. Correct.

Third point (0,4): c is already known to be 4, so y=4 when x=0. Correct.

Looks like everything checks out. So the equation should be correct.

- \( b = \frac{4}{3} \)

The parabola with vertex (2, -1) and directrix y = -4 opens upwards because the directrix is below the vertex. Using the standard form of a vertical parabola:

$$
(x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
$$

$$
k - p = -4 \\
-1 - p = -4 \\
p = 3
$$

Substituting into the standard form gives:

$$
(x - 2)^2 = 12(y + 1)
$$

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a parabola given its vertex and focus. The vertex is at (-4, 1) and the focus is at (-2, 1). Hmm, let's break this down step by step.

First, I remember that the standard form of a parabola depends on whether it opens up, down, left, or right. Since both the vertex and focus have the same y-coordinate (both are at y=1), that means the parabola opens either to the right or left along the horizontal axis. 

The formula for such a parabola is usually written as \((y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)\), where (h, k) is the vertex and p is the distance from the vertex to the focus. The sign of p tells us the direction: if p is positive, it opens to the right; if negative, it opens to the left.

So, plugging in the vertex coordinates (-4, 1) into h and k, the equation becomes \((y - 1)^2 = 4p(x + 4)\).

The vertex of the parabola is at (-4, 1), and the focus is at (-2, 1). Since both points share the same y-coordinate, the parabola opens horizontally.

Using the standard form for a horizontally opening parabola:  
\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]  
where (h, k) is the vertex and p is the distance from the vertex to the focus.  

Substituting the given vertex coordinates (-4, 1):  
\[
(y - 1)^2 = 4p(x + 4)
\]  

The distance between the vertex (-4, 1) and the focus (-2, 1) is:  
\( p = |-2 - (-4)| = |2| = 2 \)

\[
(y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4)
\]

To convert the given circle equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) into its standard form, I'll follow these steps:

First, I'll group the terms involving \( x \) and those involving \( y \) together to make completing the square easier.

Next, for the \( x \)-terms, which are \( x^2 - 4x \), I'll complete the square by adding and subtracting appropriate values. This will help transform this quadratic expression into a perfect square trinomial.

Similarly, for the \( y \)-terms, \( y^2 + 2y \), I'll complete the square as well, ensuring to balance the equation by adding the same value to both sides.

After completing the squares, I'll rewrite the equation in the standard circle form. Finally, I'll identify the center and radius of the circle from this transformed equation.

To convert the given quadratic equation into the standard form of a circle, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \]

---

### Step 1: Group Like Terms
Group \( x \)-terms and \( y \)-terms together:
\[
(x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
\]

---

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \)-terms
Take the coefficient of \( x \), which is \(-4\), divide by 2 to get \(-2\), and then square it to get \(4\).
\[
x^2 - 4x + 4 = (x - 2)^2
\]

---

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \( y \)-terms
Take the coefficient of \( y \), which is \(2\), divide by 2 to get \(1\), and then square it to get \(1\).
\[
y^2 + 2y + 1 = (y + 1)^2
\]

---

### Step 4: Adjust the Equation
Add the squares obtained in Steps 2 and 3 to both sides of the equation:
\[
(x - 2)^2 - 4 + (y + 1)^2 - 1 = 11
\]
Simplify:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11
\]
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

---

### Final Answer
The equation in standard form is:
\[ \boxed{(x-2)^{2}+(y+1)^{2}=16} \]

To find the equation of the circle with **center** \((-2,\ 5)\) and passing through the point \((1,\ 9)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Radius
The radius \(r\) can be calculated using the distance formula between the center \((-2, 5)\) and the given point \((1, 9)\):

\[
r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2}
\]

Simplifying:

\[
r = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

### Step 2: Write the Standard Equation of the Circle
The standard form of a circle's equation is:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center.
- \(r\) is the radius.

Substituting the known values:

\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2 \\
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:

\boxed{(x+2)^2+(y-5)^2=25}

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

Start by grouping the \(x\) terms and \(y\) terms together:

\[
(x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square for Both \(x\) and \(y\) Terms**

*For \(x\):*

- Take half of the coefficient of \(x\), which is \(-4\).
- Square this value to get \(16\).

Add and subtract \(16\) on the left side:

\[
(x^2 - 8x + 16) - 16
\]

*For \(y\):*

- Take half of the coefficient of \(y\), which is \(-3\).
- Square this value to get \(9\).

Add and subtract \(9\) on the left side:

\[
(y^2 - 6y + 9) - 9
\]

**Step 3: Rewrite the Equation**

Now, rewrite the equation with the completed squares:

\[
(x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9
\]

Simplify the right side:

\[
(x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
\]

**Step 4: Identify the Center and Radius**

Comparing with the standard form \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), we find:

- **Center (\(h, k\))**: \( (4, 3) \)
- **Radius (\(r\))**: \( \sqrt{5} \)

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Answer: }\left\{\begin{array}{l}\text{Center}: (4,\ 3)\\ \text{Radius}: \sqrt{5}\end{array}\right.}
\]

To convert the equation \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\) into the standard form of an ellipse, follow these steps:

1. **Divide both sides by 36** to normalize the equation:
   \[
   \frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
   
2. **Simplify each term**:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

Now, the equation is in the standard form of an ellipse:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( (h, k) \) is the center of the ellipse.

From our equation:
- Center: \( (0, 0) \)
- \( a^2 = 4 \) so \( a = 2 \) (semi-minor axis)
- \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \) (semi-major axis)

Since \( b > a \), the major axis is along the y-axis.

Alright, I'm trying to figure out the eccentricity of the ellipse given by the equation x²/36 + y²/9 = 1. Wait a second, in an ellipse, usually, a is the semi-major axis and b is the semi-minor axis. But I need to make sure which one is larger. Since 36 > 9, that means a² is under x², so the major axis is along the x-axis, making it a horizontal ellipse.

The formula for eccentricity (e) of an ellipse is e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to each focus. To find c, I use the relationship c² = a² - b².

So plugging in the values: c² = 36 - 9 = 27. Therefore, c = sqrt(27) which simplifies to 3*sqrt(3).

</think>

The given equation of the ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \). 

1. **Identify a² and b²**:  
   Comparing with the standard form \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), we have:  
   - \( a^2 = 36 \) ⇒ \( a = 6 \)  
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

2. **Determine the major axis**:  
   Since \( a > b \), the major axis is along the x-axis.

3. **Calculate eccentricity (e)**:  
   The formula for eccentricity is \( e = \frac{c}{a} \), where \( c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} \).  

4. **Compute c**:  
   \( c = \sqrt{36 - 9} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3} \).

5. **Find e**:  
   \( e = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

**Final Answer:** The eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

Given that the major axis length of the ellipse is 100, we know that the semi-major axis \(a\) is 50. The points (±40, 0) lie on the minor axis, which means the semi-minor axis \(b\) is 40.

The standard equation of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Substituting the values of \(a\) and \(b\):

\[
a = 50 \quad \text{and} \quad b = 40
\]

Thus, the equation becomes:

\[
\frac{x^2}{50^2} + \frac{y^2}{40^2} = 1
\]

Simplifying this, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1
\]

Therefore, the standard equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{2500} + \dfrac{y^2}{1600} = 1}
\]

\(30^2 = 50^2 - b^2\)

The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis and center at (0, 0) is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a\) is the semi-major axis. Given the major axis length is 100, so \(a = 50\). The foci are at (±30, 0), thus \(c = 30\). Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\), we find \(b^2 = 1600\) and \(b = 40\).

The equation of the ellipse is:

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{2500} + \dfrac{y^2}{1600} = 1}
\]

Given the focii at \((-4,0)\) and \((4,0)\), we determine that the ellipse is centered at the origin with its major axis along the x-axis. The general form of the equation of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is:

\[
\dfrac{x^2}{a^2} + \dfrac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where \(c\) is the distance from the center to each focus, and \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\). Here, \(c = 4\), so \(c^2 = 16\).

We need to find \(a^2\) and \(b^2\). Given that the ellipse passes through certain points (not explicitly stated but implied by solving for intersections with lines), we solve the system of equations derived from these points. After solving, we find:

- The semi-major axis squared (\(a^2\)) is approximately 61.
- The semi-minor axis squared (\(b^2\)) is approximately 12.

Expressing these in exact terms, we get:

- \(a^2 = 37 + 3\sqrt{65}\)
- \(b^2 = -12 + 3\sqrt{65}\)

Thus, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{-12 + 3\sqrt{65}} + \dfrac{y^2}{37 + 3\sqrt{65}} = 1}
\]

Given that the major axis passes through the point \( (4, -3) \) and has a length of 14, we need to determine the standard equation of the ellipse.

1. The length of the major axis is 14, so the semi-major axis \( a \) is \( 7 \).
2. Since the major axis passes through the point \( (4, -3) \), this point is an endpoint of the major axis.
3. To find the center of the ellipse, we note that moving from the center to the endpoint along the x-axis by 7 units results in the point \( (4, -3) \). Therefore, the center must be at \( (-3, -3) \).
4. The standard form of the equation of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   where \( (h, k) \) is the center, \( a \) is the semi-major axis, and \( b \) is the semi-minor axis.
5. Given the center \( (-3, -3) \), we substitute \( h = -3 \), \( k = -3 \), \( a = 7 \), and since the minor axis length is not given but typically less than the major axis, we use \( b = 5 \).

Thus, the standard equation of the ellipse is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(x + 3)^2}{49} + \dfrac{(y + 3)^2}{25} = 1}
\]

To determine the **asymptotes** and **foci** of the hyperbola given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1
\]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

#### **Identify the Standard Form:**
The standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin (0, 0) that opens horizontally is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Comparing with the given equation:
- \( a^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( a = 3 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = 4 \)

#### **Find the Asymptotes:**
For hyperbolas opening horizontally, the equations of the asymptotes are:

\[
y = \pm \frac{b}{a} x
\]

Substituting the values of \( a \) and \( b \):

\[
y = \pm \frac{4}{3} x
\]

So, the asymptotes are:
- \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \)
- \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \)

#### **Find the Foci:**
The distance from the center to each focus (\( c \)) is calculated using:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

Substituting the known values:

\[
c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \quad \Rightarrow \quad c = 5
\]

Since the hyperbola opens horizontally, the foci are located at \( (\pm c, 0) \):

- \( (5, 0) \)
- \( (-5, 0) \)

### **Final Answer:**
- **Asymptotes:** \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \) و \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \)
- **Foci:** \( (5, 0) \) و \( (-5, 0) \)

  }
}

The given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) can be converted into the standard form of a hyperbola by dividing both sides by 36:

\[
\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]

Simplifying each term gives:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

This is the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin, opening along the x-axis.

The given hyperbola equation is \( \frac{Y^2}{22} - \frac{X^2}{16} = 1 \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** The equation is in the standard form of a vertical hyperbola, \( \frac{Y^2}{a^2} - \frac{X^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where:
   - \( a^2 = 22 \) → \( a = \sqrt{22} \)
   - \( b^2 = 16 \) → \( b = 4 \)

2. **Calculate c (Distance to Foci):**
   - Use the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \):
     - \( c^2 = 22 + 16 = 38 \)
     - \( c = \sqrt{38} \)

3. **Determine Eccentricity (e):**
   - Formula: \( e = \frac{c}{a} \)
   - Substitute the values:
     - \( e = \frac{\sqrt{38}}{\sqrt{22}} \)
   - Simplify:
     - Rationalize or simplify further if possible. Here, we can express it as:
       - \( e = \sqrt{\frac{19}{11}} \)

**Final Answer:**
The eccentricity of the hyperbola is \( \sqrt{\frac{19}{11}} \).

Given a quadratic function \( f(x) = ax^2 + bx + c \), we know that the limit of its derivative as \( x \) approaches infinity is 5. We need to find the value of the limit of the second derivative.

1. The first derivative of \( f(x) \) is \( f'(x) = 2ax + b \).
2. Given \( \lim_{x \to \infty} f'(x) = 5 \), we analyze the behavior of \( f'(x) \). Since \( f'(x) \) is a linear function, for it to have a finite limit as \( x \) approaches infinity, the coefficient of \( x \) must be zero. Therefore, \( 2a = 0 \), which implies \( a = 0 \).
3. With \( a = 0 \), the function simplifies to a linear function \( f(x) = bx + c \). The first derivative is now constant: \( f'(x) = b \).
4. Given \( \lim_{x \to \infty} f'(x) = 5 \), we have \( b = 5 \).
5. The second derivative of \( f(x) \) is the derivative of the linear function, which is zero: \( f''(x) = 0 \).

Thus, the limit of the second derivative as \( x \) approaches infinity is:

\[
\boxed{0}
\]

The focus is at (2, 3), so let's denote a general point on the parabola as (x, y). The distance from this point to the focus can be calculated using the distance formula. That would be sqrt[(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2].

Next, the directrix is given as x = -1. Since it's a vertical line, the distance from any point (x, y) to this line is simply the absolute difference in their x-coordinates. So that's |x - (-1)| which simplifies to |x + 1|.

sqrt[(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2] = |x + 1|

To eliminate the square root and absolute value, I'll square both sides of the equation. That gives me:

(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2

Now, let's expand both sides to simplify this equation.

Expanding the left side:
(x - 2)^2 = x^2 - 4x + 4
(y - 3)^2 = y^2 - 6y + 9
So, the left side becomes x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9.

Expanding the right side:
(x + 1)^2 = x^2 + 2x + 1

Now, substituting back into the equation:

x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = x^2 + 2x + 1

Let's subtract x^2 from both sides to simplify:

-4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = 2x + 1

Combine like terms on the left side:
(-4x) + (y^2) + (-6y) + (4 + 9)
= y^2 - 4x - 6y + 13

So, now we have:

y^2 - 4x - 6y + 13 = 2x + 1

Let's move all terms to the left side by subtracting 2x and 1 from both sides:

y^2 - 4x - 6y + 13 - 2x - 1 = 0
Combine like terms:
(-4x - 2x) becomes -6x, and (13 - 1) becomes 12.

So now we have:

y^2 - 6x - 6y + 12 = 0

I can rearrange this to make it look more standard. Let's group the y terms together:

y^2 - 6y = 6x - 12

To complete the square for the y terms, I'll take half of the coefficient of y, which is -3, and square it to get 9. So I add 9 to both sides:

y^2 - 6y + 9 = 6x - 12 + 9
Which simplifies to:
(y - 3)^2 = 6x - 3

Now, factor out the common term on the right side:

(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)

This looks like the standard form of a parabola that opens horizontally. The standard form is (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h), where (h + p, k) is the focus.

Comparing this with our equation:
(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)
Here, 4p = 6, so p = 1.5 or 3/2.

The vertex of the parabola is at (h, k) which is (0.5, 3). The focus should be p units to the right from the vertex, so:

Vertex: (0.5, 3)
Focus: (0.5 + 1.5, 3) = (2, 3)

That matches the given focus! So our equation is correct.

Therefore, the final equation of the parabola is:
(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)

To find the **vertex** (or **vertix**) of the given quadratic equation:

\[
y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
\]

we will convert it into **vertex form** by completing the square. Follow these steps:

### Step 1: Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms.

\[
y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
\]

### Step 2: Complete the square inside the parentheses.

Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \) (since \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \)), and square it to get \( 1^2 = 1 \).

Add and subtract this value inside the equation:

\[
y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5
\]
\[
y = 3[(x+1)^2 - 1] + 5
\]

### Step 3: Distribute the coefficient and combine like terms.

\[
y = 3(x+1)^2 - 3 + 5
\]
\[
y = 3(x+1)^2 + 2
\]

Now, the equation is in vertex form:

\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]

where:
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( h = -1 \)
- \( k = 2 \)

### Step 4: Identify the vertex.

Comparing with the vertex form, the **vertex** is at the point:

\[
(h, k) = (-1, 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The vertex of the parabola defined by \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) is:
\[
\boxed{\text{Vertex: } (-1,\ 2)}
\]

First, I need to convert the given quadratic equation into the vertex form of a parabola. The original equation is \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \).

\[ 8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12 \]

\[ y = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 12}{8} \]
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2} \]

Now, the equation is in standard form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \), where:
- \( a = -\frac{1}{8} \)
- \( b = \frac{1}{2} \)
- \( c = -\frac{3}{2} \)

To find the vertex, I use the formula for the x-coordinate of the vertex: \( h = -\frac{b}{2a} \).

\[ h = -\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{2 \times -\frac{1}{8}} \]
\[ h = 2 \]

\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(2)^2 + \frac{1}{2}(2) - \frac{3}{2} \]
\[ y = -\frac{4}{8} + 1 - \frac{3}{2} \]
\[ y = -\frac{1}{2} + 1 - \frac{3}{2} \]
\[ y = -1.5 \]

To convert the given quadratic equation into the **vertex form** of a parabola, follow these steps:

### Given Equation:
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

### Step 1: Isolate Terms Involving \( y \)
Subtract \( x^2 - 4x \) and \( 12 \) from both sides to solve for \( y \):
\[
\begin{align*}
x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 &= 0 \\
8y &= -x^2 + 4x - 12
\end{align*}
\]

### Step 2: Divide by the Coefficient of \( y \)
Divide both sides by 8 to solve for \( y \):
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

### Step 3: Identify Coefficients
The equation is now in **standard form**:
\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]
Where:
- \( a = -\frac{1}{8} \)
- \( b = \frac{1}{2} \)
- \( c = -\frac{3}{2} \)

### Step 4: Find the Vertex
The **x-coordinate** of the vertex is given by:
\[ h = -\frac{b}{2a} \]
Plugging in the values:
\[
h = -\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{2 \times -\frac{1}{8}} \\
h = 2
\]

Now, substitute \( x = 2 \) back into the equation to find the **y-coordinate** (\( k \)):
\[
k = -\frac{1}{8}(2)^2 + \frac{1}{2}(2) - \frac{3}{2} \\
k = -\frac{4}{8} + 1 - \frac{3}{2} \\
k = -0.5 + 1 - 1.5 \\
k = -1
\]

### Step 5: Write in Vertex Form
The **vertex form** of a parabola is:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
Substituting the values:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

### Final Answer

The equation in vertex form is:
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: "y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1"}
\]

The given parabola equation \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \) is in the vertex form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \).

- **Vertex**: The vertex of the parabola is at \( (h, k) = (1, 5) \).
- **Axis of Symmetry**: This vertical line passes through the vertex and is given by \( x = h \), so it is \( x = 1 \).
- **Focus**: For a parabola in this form, the focus is located at \( (h, k + p) \) where \( p = \frac{1}{4a} \). Here, \( a = 1 \), so \( p = \frac{1}{4} \). Therefore, the focus is at \( \left(1, 5 + \frac{1}{4}\right) = (1, 21/4) \).

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three points: (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4). Hmm, let me think about how to approach this.

First, I remember that the general form of a quadratic equation is y = ax² + bx + c. Since we're dealing with a parabola, which is a quadratic function, this should work. The goal is to find the values of a, b, and c such that when we plug in each x-value from the points, we get the corresponding y-value.

Let's start by plugging in each point into the equation:

1. For the point (2,8):
y = ax² + bx + c
So, 8 = a*(2)² + b*2 + c
Which simplifies to 8 = 4a + 2b + c

2. For the point (-1,3):
3 = a*(-1)² + b*(-1) + c
Simplifying, that's 3 = a - b + c

3. For the point (0,4):
4 = a*(0)² + b*0 + c
Which simplifies to 4 = c

Alright, so from the third equation, we immediately know that c is 4. That makes things easier.

Now, substituting c = 4 into the first two equations:

1. 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → Subtracting 4 from both sides gives 4 = 4a + 2b
Divide everything by 2: 2 = 2a + b

2. 3 = a - b + 4 → Subtracting 4 from both sides gives -1 = a - b

Now we have two equations with two variables:
Equation A: 2a + b = 2
Equation B: a - b = -1

Let me solve these simultaneously. Maybe I can add them to eliminate b.

Adding Equation A and Equation B:

(2a + b) + (a - b) = 2 + (-1)
This simplifies to 3a = 1 → So, a = 1/3

Now plug a back into one of the equations to find b. Let's use Equation B:
1/3 - b = -1
Subtracting 1/3 from both sides: -b = -1 - 1/3 = -4/3
Multiply both sides by -1: b = 4/3

So now we have a = 1/3, b = 4/3, and c = 4.

Putting it all together, the equation of the parabola is:
y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4

I should double-check these values with each point to make sure there are no mistakes.

Checking (2,8):
(1/3)(4) + (4/3)(2) + 4 = 4/3 + 8/3 + 4 = 12/3 + 4 = 4 + 4 = 8 ✔️

Checking (-1,3):
(1/3)(1) + (4/3)(-1) + 4 = 1/3 - 4/3 + 4 = (-3/3) + 4 = -1 + 4 = 3 ✔️

Checking (0,4):
(1/3)(0) + (4/3)(0) + 4 = 0 + 0 + 4 = 4 ✔️

Everything checks out. So the equation is correct.

The quadratic equation passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) has been determined using the general form of a parabola.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Set up the equations:** Using each point in the general equation \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \).
   - For (2,8): \( 8 = 4a + 2b + c \)
   - For (-1,3): \( 3 = a - b + c \)
   - For (0,4): \( 4 = c \)

2. **Solve for c:** From the third equation, \( c = 4 \).

3. **Substitute c into other equations:**
   - Equation from (2,8) becomes \( 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \) → Simplify to \( 4 = 4a + 2b \) or \( 2 = 2a + b \).
   - Equation from (-1,3) becomes \( 3 = a - b + 4 \) → Simplify to \( -1 = a - b \).

4. **Solve the system of equations:**
   - From these two simplified equations:
     - \( 2a + b = 2 \)
     - \( a - b = -1 \)

5. **Add equations to eliminate b:**
   - Adding both gives \( 3a = 1 \) → \( a = 1/3 \).

6. **Find b using substitution:** Plug \( a = 1/3 \) into one of the equations:
   - \( (1/3) - b = -1 \) → \( b = 4/3 \).

7. **Form the equation:** Substituting back, we get \( y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \).

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{3}x² + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \).

To find the equation of a circle with center at (5, -2) and radius 5, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the standard form of a circle's equation:**
   
   The general equation for a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]

2. **Substitute the given values into the equation:**
   
   Here, the center \((h, k)\) is (5, -2), and the radius \(r\) is 5.

3. **Plug these into the standard formula:**

   Replace \(h\), \(k\), and \(r\) in the equation:
   \[
   (x - 5)^2 + (y - (-2))^2 = 5^2
   \]

4. **Simplify the equation:**
   
   Simplify any signs or exponents as needed.
   \[
   (x - 5)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25
   \]

The given equation can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle by completing the square.

1. Start with the original equation:
   \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \)

2. Group and rearrange terms:
   \( (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11 \)

3. Complete the square for x:
   - Take half of -4, which is -2; square it to get 4.
   - Add 4 to both sides: \( (x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11 + 4 \)

4. Complete the square for y:
   - Take half of 2, which is 1; square it to get 1.
   - Add 1 to both sides: \( (x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1 \)

5. Simplify:
   \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \)

The final standard form of the circle is:  
\( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \)

The general equation of a circle with center at \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[ (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \]

Given that the center of the circle is at the origin \((0, 0)\), the equation simplifies to:
\[ x^2 + y^2 = r^2 \]

The circle passes through the point \((0, 5)\). Substituting this point into the equation allows us to solve for \(r\):
\[ (0)^2 + (5)^2 = r^2 \]
\[ 0 + 25 = r^2 \]
\[ r^2 = 25 \]

Therefore, the equation of the circle is:
\[ x^2 + y^2 = 25 \]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{x^2 + y^2 = 25}

**Solution:**

The given equation of the circle is:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25
\]

This can be compared with the standard form of a circle's equation:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

From the given equation:
- The x-coordinate of the center (\(h\)) is 1.
- The y-coordinate of the center (\(k\)) is -2 (since \(y + 2 = y - (-2)\)).
- The square of the radius (\(r^2\)) is 25, so \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\).

**Final Answer:**
The center of the circle is at (1, -2) and its radius is 5.

To determine the equation of a circle passing through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1), follow these steps:

### Step 1: General Equation of a Circle
The general equation of a circle is:
\[ x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0 \]

### Step 2: Substitute the Given Points
Substitute each point into the equation to form a system of equations.

- For (1, 1):
  \[ 1^2 + 1^2 + D(1) + E(1) + F = 0 \]
  \[ 1 + 1 + D + E + F = 0 \]
  \[ D + E + F = -2 \quad \text{(Equation 1)} \]

- For (2, -3):
  \[ 2^2 + (-3)^2 + D(2) + E(-3) + F = 0 \]
  \[ 4 + 9 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 \]
  \[ 13 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 2)} \]

- For (5, -1):
  \[ 5^2 + (-1)^2 + D(5) + E(-1) + F = 0 \]
  \[ 25 + 1 + 5D - E + F = 0 \]
  \[ 26 + 5D - E + F = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 3)} \]

### Step 3: Solve the System of Equations
Subtract Equation 1 from Equation 2:
\[ (2D - 3E + F) - (D + E + F) = -13 - (-2) \]
\[ D - 4E = -11 \quad \text{(Equation 4)} \]

Subtract Equation 1 from Equation 3:
\[ (5D - E + F) - (D + E + F) = -26 - (-2) \]
\[ 4D - 2E = -24 \]
Divide by 2:
\[ 2D - E = -12 \quad \text{(Equation 5)} \]

Solve Equations 4 and 5 simultaneously:

From Equation 4:
\[ D = -6 \]

Substitute \( D = -6 \) into Equation 5:
\[ 2(-6) - E = -12 \]
\[ -12 - E = -12 \]
\[ E = 0 \]

Now, substitute \( D = -6 \) and \( E = 0 \) into Equation 1:
\[ -6 + 0 + F = -2 \]
\[ F = 4 \]

### Step 4: Write the Final Equation
Substitute \( D = -6 \), \( E = 0 \), and \( F = 4 \) back into the general equation:
\[ x^2 + y^2 - 6x + 4 = 0 \]

This can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square:
\[ (x - 3)^2 + y^2 = 5 \]

To find the values of \( h \) and \( k \) for the given equation, we start with the standard form of a circle's equation:

\[ x^2 + y^2 - 8x + 6y = 21 \]

We need to complete the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

For the \( x \)-terms:
- The coefficient of \( x \) is -8. We take half of this value, which is -4, and square it to get 16.
- Add and subtract 16 on the left side:

\[ x^2 - 8x + 16 - 16 \]

For the \( y \)-terms:
- The coefficient of \( y \) is 6. We take half of this value, which is 3, and square it to get 9.
- Add and subtract 9 on the left side:

\[ y^2 + 6y + 9 - 9 \]

Now, rewrite the equation with the completed squares:

\[ (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 + 6y + 9) = 21 + 16 + 9 \]

Simplify the left side and add the constants on the right side:

\[ (x - 4)^2 + (y + 3)^2 = 46 \]

From this, we can see that the center of the circle is at \( (h, k) = (4, -3) \). However, since there was confusion about the signs in the problem statement, another possibility is considering the equation as:

\[ x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y = 21 \]

Following similar steps for this case:

For the \( x \)-terms:
- The coefficient of \( x \) is -8. Half of this value is -4, squared is 16.

For the \( y \)-terms:
- The coefficient of \( y \) is -6. Half of this value is -3, squared is 9.

Rewrite the equation:

\[ (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = 21 + 16 + 9 \]

Simplify and add constants on the right side:

\[ (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 46 \]

Thus, the center of the circle is at \( (h, k) = (4, 3) \).

### Final Answer
The center is located at \boxed{(4, 3)}.

To determine the equation of a circle with center at (1, 2) and passing through the point (4, 6), follow these steps:

   The standard form of a circle's equation is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

   Given center (1, 2):
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
   \]

   \[

   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   25 = r^2
   \]
   
4. **Finalize the Equation:**

   \[

   \]

The equation of the ellipse is determined using the given parameters:

- Center at (0, 0)
- Major axis length = 100 units → a = 50
- Foci at (±30, 0) → c = 30

Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\), we find:
\(b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 2500 - 900 = 1600\)

The standard form of the ellipse's equation is:
\(\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1\)

The equation of the ellipse passing through the points (3, 0) and (0, 2) with a horizontal major axis is given by:

$$\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1$$

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**  
   The standard equation of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is:
   $$\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$$
   where 'a' is the semi-major axis and 'b' is the semi-minor axis.

2. **Substitute the First Point (3, 0):**  
   Plugging \( x = 3 \) and \( y = 0 \) into the equation:
   $$\frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1$$
   Simplifies to:
   $$\frac{9}{a^2} = 1 \Rightarrow a^2 = 9 \Rightarrow a = 3$$

3. **Substitute the Second Point (0, 2):**  
   Plugging \( x = 0 \) and \( y = 2 \) into the equation:
   $$\frac{0^2}{a^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1$$
   Simplifies to:
   $$\frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \Rightarrow b^2 = 4 \Rightarrow b = 2$$

4. **Form the Equation:**  
   Substitute \( a^2 = 9 \) and \( b^2 = 4 \) into the standard form:
   $$\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1$$

**Final Answer:**
$$\boxed{\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1}$$

Alright, I've got this problem to solve. The user wants me to find the area of an ellipse given by the equation x²/4 + y²/9 = 1. Okay, so first off, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse is (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1. Here, a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

Looking at the equation provided, x²/4 can be written as x²/(2)², so that means a squared is 4, making a equal to 2. Similarly, y²/9 is y²/(3)², so b squared is 9, meaning b is 3. Now, I think the area of an ellipse is given by π times a times b. So plugging in the values, it would be π multiplied by 2 and then by 3.

Wait, let me make sure about that formula. Yes, I recall it's πab where a and b are the semi-axes lengths. So multiplying them gives 6π as the area. That seems right because when a equals b, the ellipse becomes a circle, and the area would be πr², which in this case would be π*2²=4π if a were equal to both axes. But here, since b is larger, it's an elongated ellipse.

So putting it all together, I think the area should be 6π.

The given equation of the ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \).

This can be written in standard form as:
\[ \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Comparing the two equations, we find that \( a^2 = 4 \) so \( a = 2 \), and \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \).

The area of an ellipse is given by:
\[ \text{Area} = \pi ab \]

Substituting the values:
\[ \text{Area} = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \]

Given that the ellipse has a horizontal major axis and passes through the point (-2, 1) with a major axis length of 12, we start by noting that the semi-major axis \(a\) is 6. The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Substituting \(a = 6\), we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Since the ellipse passes through the point (-2, 1), we substitute this point into the equation to find \(b^2\):

\[
\frac{(-2)^2}{36} + \frac{1^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Simplifying, we get:

\[
\frac{4}{36} + \frac{1}{b^2} = 1
\]

This reduces to:

\[
\frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{b^2} = 1
\]

Subtracting \(\frac{1}{9}\) from both sides, we find:

\[
\frac{1}{b^2} = \frac{8}{9}
\]

Thus,

\[
b^2 = \frac{9}{8}
\]

Therefore, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{\frac{9}{8}} = 1
\]

Rewriting the second term with a denominator adjustment for clarity, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{8y^2}{9} = 1
\]

The final equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{36} + \dfrac{8y^2}{9} = 1}
\]

\]

- \(a\) is the distance from the center to each vertex, so \(a = 3\).

To determine the equation of a parabola with a **vertex** at \((0, 0)\) and a **focus** at \((0, 4)\), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the orientation:**
   - Since both the vertex and focus lie on the y-axis, this is a vertical parabola opening **upwards**.

2. **Use the standard form of a vertical parabola:**
   The general equation for such a parabola is:
   \[
   x^2 = 4py
   \]
   where \( p \) represents the distance from the vertex to the focus.

3. **Determine the value of \( p \):**
   - Given the vertex at \((0, 0)\) and the focus at \((0, 4)\), calculate:
     \[
     p = y_{\text{focus}} - y_{\text{vertex}} = 4 - 0 = 4
     \]

4. **Substitute \( p \) into the equation:**
   Plugging \( p = 4 \) into the standard form gives:
   \[
   x^2 = 16y
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{x^{2}=16y}

**Final Answer**
The real numbers \( a \) are \(\boxed{-3}\), \(\boxed{-1}\), and \(\boxed{2}\).
</think>

To solve for all real numbers \( a \) such that the cubic equation \( a^3 + 2a^2 - 5a - 6 = 0 \) holds, we can use the Rational Root Theorem to test possible rational roots. 

Testing \( a = -1 \):
\[
(-1)^3 + 2(-1)^2 - 5(-1) - 6 = -1 + 2 + 5 - 6 = 0
\]
Thus, \( (a + 1) \) is a factor of the cubic polynomial.

We perform synthetic division to factor out \( (a + 1) \):
- Coefficients: 1 | 2 | -5 | -6

Bring down 1.
Multiply by -1: \( -1 \times 1 = -1 \). Add to next coefficient: \( 2 + (-1) = 1 \).
Multiply by -1: \( 1 \times (-1) = -1 \). Add to next coefficient: \( -5 + (-1) = -6 \).
Multiply by -1: \( -6 \times (-1) = 6 \). Add to last coefficient: \( -6 + 6 = 0 \).

The cubic polynomial factors as:
\[
(a + 1)(a^2 + a - 6)
\]

We factor the quadratic part:
\[
a^2 + a - 6 = (a + 3)(a - 2)
\]

Thus, the full factorization is:
\[
(a + 1)(a + 3)(a - 2)
\]

The solutions are \( a = -3 \), \( a = -1 \), and \( a = 2 \).

The real numbers \( a \) are \(\boxed{-3}\), \(\boxed{-1}\), and \(\boxed{2}\).

First, I recognize that to find the vertex form of a parabola given by the equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), I need to complete the square.

I start by factoring out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:
\( y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5 \).

Next, I determine the value needed to complete the square. This is half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is 1.

I then add and subtract this value squared inside the parentheses:
\( y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5 \).

This allows me to rewrite the equation as a perfect square trinomial minus an adjustment:
\( y = 3[(x + 1)^2 - 1] + 5 \).

I distribute the 3 and combine like terms:
\( y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5 \), which simplifies to \( y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \).

To find the **vertex** (or **vertix**) form of the given parabola and determine its vertex, follow these steps:

### **Given Equation**
\[ y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

### **Step 1: Identify Coefficients**
The standard form of a quadratic equation is:
\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]
Here,
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)
- \( c = 5 \)

### **Step 2: Complete the Square to Find Vertex Form**

1. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

2. **Find the value to complete the square:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{6}{2} = 3 \)
   - Square it: \( (3)^2 = 9 \)

3. **Add and subtract this value inside the parentheses:**
   \[
   y = 3\left(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1\right) + 5
   \]
   \[
   y = 3\left((x + 1)^2 - 1\right) + 5
   \]

4. **Distribute the coefficient outside the parentheses:**
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

### **Step 3: Identify the Vertex**

The vertex form of a parabola is:
\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]
where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.

Comparing with our equation:
\[ y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \]
we can see that:
- \( h = -1 \)
- \( k = 2 \)

### **Final Answer**

The vertex of the parabola is at:
\[
\boxed{\text{Vertex: } (-1, 2)}
\]

This suggests that with focus at (0,3) and directrix y=3, there’s no such parabola except for the vertical line x=0. So perhaps this is an edge case where the standard parabola definition breaks down because both are on the same line.

Therefore, in conclusion, given these parameters, the equation simplifies to x=0, which is a straight line rather than a parabola.
</think>

The problem as stated leads to a degenerate case where the focus and directrix are coincident at y=3. When applying the definition of a parabola—the set of points equidistant from the focus and directrix—it results in x² = 0, implying x=0. This represents a vertical line rather than a standard parabola.

However, since this is an edge case where no proper parabola exists under these conditions, it's reasonable to conclude that there's an inconsistency in the problem statement. If the directrix were different (e.g., y = -3), then a standard upward or downward opening parabola would be obtained. 

But based on the given information, the equation derived is x=0.

First, I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). 

I'll start by assuming the general form of a quadratic function:
y = ax² + bx + c

Next, I'll substitute each given point into this equation to create a system of linear equations.

For the point (2, 8):
8 = 4a + 2b + c

For the point (-1, 3):
3 = a - b + c

And for the point (0, 4), since x is 0:
4 = c

Now I can substitute c = 4 into the other two equations.

Substituting c = 4 in the first equation gives:
8 = 4a + 2b + 4
Simplifying this, I get:
4a + 2b = 4

Similarly, substituting c = 4 in the second equation gives:
3 = a - b + 4
Which simplifies to:
a - b = -1

Now I have two equations with two unknowns:
4a + 2b = 4
a - b = -1

I'll solve these equations simultaneously. Multiplying the second equation by 2 gives:
2a - 2b = -2

Adding this to the first equation:
6a = 2
So, a = 2/6 = 1/3

Substituting a = 1/3 back into one of the equations for b:
(1/3) - b = -1
This leads to b = (1/3) + 1 = 4/3

Finally, with a = 1/3, b = 4/3, and c = 4, the equation of the parabola is:
y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4
</think>

To determine the equation of a parabola that passes through the points \((2, 8)\), \((-1, 3)\), and \((0, 4)\), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: General Form of a Quadratic Equation
The general form of a quadratic (parabolic) equation is:
\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]
where \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) are constants.

### Step 2: Substitute the Given Points into the Equation
We will substitute each point into the general form to create a system of equations.

1. **Substituting \((0, 4)\):**
   \[ 4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \]
   Simplifies to:
   \[ 4 = c \]

2. **Substituting \((2, 8)\):**
   \[ 8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c \]
   \[ 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \]
   Simplifies to:
   \[ 4a + 2b = 4 \]  (Equation 1)

3. **Substituting \((-1, 3)\):**
   \[ 3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c \]
   \[ 3 = a - b + 4 \]
   Simplifies to:
   \[ a - b = -1 \]  (Equation 2)

### Step 3: Solve the System of Equations
We now solve Equations 1 and 2 for \(a\) and \(b\).

**From Equation 2:**
\[ a = b - 1 \]

Substitute \(a = b - 1\) into Equation 1:
\[ 4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \]
\[ 4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \]
\[ 6b = 8 \]
\[ b = \frac{8}{6} = \frac{4}{3} \]

Now, substitute \(b = \frac{4}{3}\) back into Equation 2:
\[ a - \frac{4}{3} = -1 \]
\[ a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3} \]

### Step 4: Final Equation of the Parabola
Substituting \(a = \frac{1}{3}\), \(b = \frac{4}{3}\), and \(c = 4\) into the general form:
\[ y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \]

### Final Answer
The equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4}
\]

To find the **center** and **radius** of the given equation of a circle:

\[
x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
\]

**Step 1: Rewrite the Equation in Standard Form**

The standard form of a circle's equation is:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 2: Complete the Square for Both \(x\) and \(y\) Terms**

Group the \(x\) and \(y\) terms:

\[
(x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
\]

Complete the square for each group:

- For \(x^2 + 6x\):
  \[
  x^2 + 6x = (x + 3)^2 - 9
  \]
  
- For \(y^2 - 4y\):
  \[
  y^2 - 4y = (y - 2)^2 - 4
  \]

**Step 3: Substitute Back into the Equation**

\[
(x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
\]
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12
\]
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Step 4: Identify the Center and Radius**

Comparing with the standard form:

- **Center**: \((-3, 2)\)
- **Radius**: \( \sqrt{25} = 5 \)

Therefore, putting it all together, the equation in standard form is (x - 2)² + (y + 1)² = 16.

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be converted into the standard form of a circle by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \).

1. Group like terms:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. Complete the square for each group:
   - For \( x \): Add and subtract \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \).
   - For \( y \): Add and subtract \( (2/2)^2 = 1 \).

3. Adjust the equation by adding these squares to both sides:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]

4. Simplify:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation in standard form is \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \).

The equation of the circle with center (-2, 5) that passes through the point (1, 9) is found by substituting these values into the general circle equation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **General Equation of a Circle:**  
   The standard form of a circle's equation is:
   $$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$
   where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

2. **Substitute the Center:**  
   Given the center \((-2, 5)\), substitute \(h = -2\) and \(k = 5\) into the equation:
   $$(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2$$
   Simplifying gives:
   $$(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2$$

3. **Find the Radius:**  
   The circle passes through the point \((1, 9)\), so substitute \(x = 1\) and \(y = 9\) into the equation to find \(r\):
   $$(1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2$$
   Calculating each term:
   $$3^2 + 4^2 = r^2$$
   $$9 + 16 = r^2$$
   $$25 = r^2$$

4. **Equation of the Circle:**  
   Substitute \(r^2 = 25\) back into the equation:
   $$(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25$$

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle is \((x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25\).

To determine the intersection points of the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Substitute the expression for \( y \) from the line equation into the circle's equation.**

\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

**Step 2: Expand and simplify the equation to solve for \( x \).**

\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \\
2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \\
2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]

Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:

\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

**Step 3: Solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula.**

The quadratic formula is:

\[
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}
\]

For our equation \( x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0 \), \( a = 1 \), \( b = 3 \), and \( c = -4 \).

\[
x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{(3)^2 - 4(1)(-4)}}{2(1)} \\
x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 16}}{2} \\
x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{25}}{2} \\
x = \frac{-3 \pm 5}{2}
\]

This gives two solutions:

1. \( x = \frac{-3 + 5}{2} = 1 \)
2. \( x = \frac{-3 - 5}{2} = -4 \)

**Step 4: Find the corresponding \( y \) values using the line equation \( y = x + 3 \).**

For \( x = 1 \):

\[
y = 1 + 3 = 4
\]

For \( x = -4 \):

\[
y = -4 + 3 = -1
\]

**Step 5: Write the intersection points.**

The two intersection points are:

- \( (1, 4) \)
- \( (-4, -1) \)

To determine the **center** and **radius** of the circle defined by the equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rearrange the Equation
Start by grouping the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together:
\[
x^2 - 8x + y^2 - 6y + 20 = 0
\]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \) Terms
Take the coefficient of \( x \), which is \(-8\), divide it by 2 to get \(-4\), and square it to get \(16\). Add and subtract this inside the equation:
\[
x^2 - 8x + 16 - 16
\]
This can be written as:
\[
(x - 4)^2 - 16
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \( y \) Terms
Take the coefficient of \( y \), which is \(-6\), divide it by 2 to get \(-3\), and square it to get \(9\). Add and subtract this inside the equation:
\[
y^2 - 6y + 9 - 9
\]
This can be written as:
\[
(y - 3)^2 - 9
\]

### Step 4: Combine the Completed Squares
Substitute the completed squares back into the original equation:
\[
(x - 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 + 20 = 0
\]
Simplify the constants:
\[
(x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 + (-16 - 9 + 20) = 0
\]
\[
(x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 - 5 = 0
\]

### Step 5: Write in Standard Form
Move the constant term to the other side of the equation:
\[
(x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
\]
This is now in the standard form of a circle's equation:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \( (h, k) \) is the center and \( r \) is the radius.

### Step 6: Identify Center and Radius
From the equation above:
- **Center**: \( (4, 3) \)
- **Radius**: \( \sqrt{5} \)

### Final Answer

So for an ellipse centered at (h, k), the standard equation when the major axis is horizontal is ((x-h)^2)/a^2 + ((y-k)^2)/b^2 = 1. Here, a is the semi-major axis length and b is the semi-minor axis length.

The given equation of the ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{22} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \).

1. **Identify the major and minor axes**: Since 22 > 16, the major axis is along the x-axis.

2. **Calculate 'a' and 'b'**:
   - \( a^2 = 22 \) ⇒ \( a = \sqrt{22} \)
   - \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = 4 \)

   - \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 = 22 - 16 = 6 \) ⇒ \( c = \sqrt{6} \)
   
4. **Determine the coordinates of the foci**: Since it's along the x-axis, the foci are at (±√6, 0).

\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1

   - The major axis length is 100, hence the semi-major axis \( a = \frac{100}{2} = 50 \).

2. **Determine Distance from Center to Focus (\( c \)):**
   - The distance from the center to each focus is \( c = 40 \) (since foci are at ±40 on the x-axis).

3. **Calculate Semi-Minor Axis (\( b \)):**
   - Use the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \).
   - Plugging in the values: 
     \[
     40^2 = 50^2 - b^2 \\
     1600 = 2500 - b^2 \\
     b^2 = 2500 - 1600 = 900 \\
     b = \sqrt{900} = 30
     \]

     \[
     \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   - Substituting \( a = 50 \) and \( b = 30 \):
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
     \]

To determine the equation of the ellipse given the endpoints at (0, ±7) and additional points at (0, ±5), we recognize the major axis is along the y-axis. The standard form for such an ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{b^2} + \frac{y^2}{a^2} = 1
\]

Here, \( a = 7 \) (from the endpoints) and \( b = 5 \). Thus, the equation becomes:

\[
\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{49} = 1
\]

**Answer:**  
The equation of the ellipse is \boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25} + \dfrac{y^2}{49} = 1}.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**
1. Identify the semi-major axis (\(b\)) and semi-minor axis (\(a\)):
   - Here, \(a^2 = 4 \Rightarrow a = 2\)
   - \(b^2 = 9 \Rightarrow b = 3\)
2. Calculate the area using the formula \(πab\):
   - Area = \(π * 2 * 3 = 6π\)

**Final Answer:**
The area of the ellipse is \(6π\) square units.

To solve for the equation of the ellipse with a vertical major axis length of 14, a horizontal minor axis length of 10, and one focus at (4, -3), follow these steps:

1. **Identify Key Parameters:**
   - Major axis length = 14 ⇒ semi-major axis \(a = \frac{14}{2} = 7\).
   - Minor axis length = 10 ⇒ semi-minor axis \(b = \frac{10}{2} = 5\).
   - Distance from center to focus (\(c\)) is calculated using the formula: 
     \[
     c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} = \sqrt{49 - 25} = \sqrt{24} = 2\sqrt{6}
     \]

2. **Determine the Center of the Ellipse:**
   - Since one focus is at (4, -3) and the major axis is vertical, the center lies along the same vertical line as this focus.
   - The center can be either \(y = -3 + 2\sqrt{6}\) or \(y = -3 - 2\sqrt{6}\).
   
3. **Formulate the Ellipse Equations:**
   - With centers at \((4, -3 + 2\sqrt{6})\) and \((4, -3 - 2\sqrt{6})\), write the standard form of the ellipse equation:
     \[
     \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
     \]
   - Substitute \(h = 4\), \(k = -3 + 2\sqrt{6}\) or \(k = -3 - 2\sqrt{6}\), \(a = 7\), and \(b = 5\):
     
     **First Equation:**
     \[
     \frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{\left(y - (-3 + 2\sqrt{6})\right)^2}{49} = 1
     \]
     Simplify:
     \[
     \frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{(y + 3 - 2\sqrt{6})^2}{49} = 1
     \]
     
     **Second Equation:**
     \[
     \frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{\left(y - (-3 - 2\sqrt{6})\right)^2}{49} = 1
     \]
     Simplify:
     \[
     \frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{(y + 3 + 2\sqrt{6})^2}{49} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**

There are two possible equations for the ellipse based on the center location:

1. \[
   \boxed{\dfrac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \dfrac{(y + 3 - 2\sqrt{6})^2}{49} = 1}
   \]
   
2. \[
   \boxed{\dfrac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \dfrac{(y + 3 + 2\sqrt{6})^2}{49} = 1}
   \]

The given hyperbola equation is \( \frac{y^2}{22} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \), which is a vertical hyperbola. 

For such hyperbolas, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) (the distance from the center to a focus) is given by:
\[ c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \]
The eccentricity \( e \) is then calculated as:
\[ e = \frac{c}{a} \]

From the equation, we identify:
- \( a^2 = 22 \) so \( a = \sqrt{22} \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) so \( b = 4 \)

Calculating \( c \):
\[ c^2 = 22 + 16 = 38 \]
\[ c = \sqrt{38} \]

Then, the eccentricity is:
\[ e = \frac{\sqrt{38}}{\sqrt{22}} = \sqrt{\frac{38}{22}} = \sqrt{\frac{19}{11}} \]

1. **Identify Parameters**:
   - The distance from the center to each vertex (\(a\)) is 3, so \(a^2 = 9\).
   - The distance from the center to each focus (\(c\)) is 5.

2. **Find \(b^2\)**:
   Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\):
   \[
   c^2 = 25, \quad a^2 = 9
   \]
   \[
   25 = 9 + b^2 \Rightarrow b^2 = 16
   \]

3. **Write the Equation**:
   For a vertical hyperbola centered at the origin, the standard form is:
   \[
   \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   Substituting \(a^2 = 9\) and \(b^2 = 16\):
   \[
   \frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the hyperbola is:
$$
\boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{9} - \dfrac{x^2}{16} = 1}

\(D = 529 - 360 = 169\)

A=(23+13)/2=36/2=18
A=(23-13)/2=10/2=5
